\rank(\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{X};Y})=1
,
f_{1}^{*}(\underline{x})\propto\tanh(\underline{\mu}^{\mathrm{T}}\Sigma^{-1}\underline{x})
, and
g_{1}^{*}(y)=y
.
∎

From Example
40
,
the maximal correlation function
f_{1}^{*}
can be represented
by a
d\times 1
linear layer activated by
\tanh(\cdot)
, with zero bias and weight
\Sigma^{-1}\underline{\mu}
.

A.2
Finite Alphabets: Information Vector and Canonical Dependence Matrix

For finite data alphabets, it can be convenient to introduce the vector and matrix representations of features. To begin, we assume the random variable
Z
takes finite many possible values, i.e.,
|\mathcal{Z}|<\infty
, then the resulting feature space
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is a finite-dimensional vector space. It is sometimes more convenient to represent features using vector and matrix notations. Specifically, each
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Z}}
can be equivalently expressed as one vector in
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Z}|}
as follows. Suppose
R_{Z}
is the metric distribution, then we can construct an orthonormal basis
\left\{\mathsf{b}_{Z}^{(z)}\colon z\in\mathcal{Z}\right\}
of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, where

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}_{Z}^{(z)}(z^{\prime})\triangleq\frac{\delta_{zz^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{R_{Z}(z)}}\quad\text{for all $z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}$}.

(117)

We refer to this basis as the
canonical basis
of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Z}}
. For all
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, we can represent
f
as a linear combination of these basis functions, i.e.,

\displaystyle f=\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}}\xi(z^{\prime})\cdot\mathsf{b}_{Z}^{(z^{\prime})},

where the coefficient
\xi(z)
for each
z\in\mathcal{Z}
is given by

\displaystyle\xi(z)=\left\langle f,\mathsf{b}_{Z}^{(z)}\right\rangle=f(z)\sqrt{R_{Z}(z)}.

(118)

In particular, when
f
is the density ratio
\tilde{\ell}_{P_{Z}}
for some
P_{Z}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, the corresponding coefficient
\xi(z)
for each
z\in\mathcal{Z}
is

\displaystyle\xi(z)=\left\langle\tilde{\ell}_{P_{Z}},\mathsf{b}_{Z}^{(z)}\right\rangle=\frac{P_{Z}(z)-R_{Z}(z)}{\sqrt{R_{Z}(z)}}.

(119)

This establishes a one-to-one correspondence between
\tilde{\ell}_{P_{Z}}
(or
P_{Z}
) and the vector
\underline{\xi}\triangleq[\xi(z),z\in\mathcal{Z}]^{\mathrm{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Z}|}
, which is referred to as the information vector associated with
\tilde{\ell}_{P_{Z}}
(or
P_{Z}
).

Similarly, for
X
and
Y
with
|\mathcal{X}|<\infty,|\mathcal{Y}|<\infty
, we can represent the CDK function
\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}
as
an
|\mathcal{X}|\times|\mathcal{Y}|
matrix
\tilde{B}_{X;Y}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{B}_{X;Y}(x;y)

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{P_{X,Y}(x,y)-P_{X}(x)P_{Y}(y)}{\sqrt{P_{X}(x)}\sqrt{P_{Y}(y)}},

(120)

which is referred to as the canonical dependence matrix (CDM) of
X
and
Y
. With the metric distribution
P_{X}P_{Y}
on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}
, each
\tilde{B}_{X;Y}(x;y)
is the coefficient associated with the basis function
\mathsf{b}_{X,Y}^{(x,y)}
[cf. (
117
)].

In addition, we have
\rank(\tilde{B}_{X;Y})=\rank(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y})
. Suppose
\sigma_{i}(f_{i}^{*}\otimes g_{i}^{*})=\md_{i}(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y})
, for each
1\leq i\leq\rank(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y})
, then
\sigma_{i}
is the
i
-th singular vector of
\tilde{B}_{X;Y}
, and the corresponding
i
-th left and right singular vector pair are given by
\underline{\xi}_{i}^{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{X}|},\underline{\xi}_{i}^{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}
, with [cf. (
118
)]

\displaystyle\xi_{i}^{X}(x)=\sqrt{P_{X}(x)}\cdot f_{i}^{*}(x),\quad\xi_{i}^{Y}(y)=\sqrt{P_{Y}(y)}\cdot g_{i}^{*}(y).

(121)

Therefore, for small-scale discrete data, we can use the connection (
121
) to obtain the modal decomposition by solving the SVD of the corresponding CDM.

Appendix B
Characterization of Common Optimal Solutions

We consider optimization problems defined on a common domain
\mathcal{D}
. Specifically, given
k
functions
h_{i}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,\dots,k
, let us consider optimization problems

\displaystyle\maximize_{u\in\mathcal{D}}h_{i}(u),\quad i=1,\dots,k.

(122)

For each
i=1,\dots,k
, we denote the optimal solution and optimal value for
i
-th problem by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}\triangleq\argmax_{u\in\mathcal{D}}h_{i}(u),\quad t_{i}^{*}\triangleq\max_{u\in\mathcal{D}}h_{i}(u),

(123)

respectively, and suppose
\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}\neq\varnothing,i=1,\dots,k
.

Then, the set
\mathcal{D}^{*}\triangleq\cap_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}
represents the collection of common optimal solutions for all
k
optimization problems (
122
). When such common solutions exist, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}^{*}
is nonempty, we can obtain
\mathcal{D}^{*}
by a single optimization program, by using an objective that aggregates original
k
objectives.
We formalize the result as follows.

Proposition 41
.

If
\mathcal{D}^{*}\neq\varnothing
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{*}=\argmax_{u\in\mathcal{D}}\,\Gamma(h_{1}(u),h_{2}(u),\dots,h_{k}(u))

for every
\Gamma\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}

that is strictly increasing in each argument.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{D}^{**}\triangleq\argmax_{u\in\mathcal{D}}\,h(u)
with
h(u)\triangleq\Gamma(h_{1}(u),h_{2}(u),\dots,h_{k}(u))
. Then the proposition is equivalent to
\mathcal{D}^{*}=\mathcal{D}^{**}
. We then establish
\mathcal{D}^{*}\subset\mathcal{D}^{**}
and
\mathcal{D}^{**}\subset\mathcal{D}^{*}
, respectively.

To prove
\mathcal{D}^{*}\subset\mathcal{D}^{**}
, take any
u^{*}\in\mathcal{D}^{*}
. Then, for all
u\in\mathcal{D}
, we have
h_{i}(u)\leq h_{i}(u^{*})
,
i=1,\dots,k
, which implies that

\displaystyle h(u)=\Gamma(h_{1}(u),\dots,h_{k}(u))\leq\Gamma(h_{1}(u^{*}),\dots,h_{k}(u^{*}))=h(u^{*}).

Therefore, we have
u^{*}\in\mathcal{D}^{**}
. Since
u^{*}
can be arbitrarily chosen from
\mathcal{D}^{*}
, we have
\mathcal{D}^{*}\subset\mathcal{D}^{**}
.

We then establish
\mathcal{D}^{**}\subset\mathcal{D}^{*}
, which is equivalent to

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{*})\subset(\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{**}).

(124)

Note that (
124
) is trivially true if
\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{*}=\varnothing
. Otherwise, take any
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{*}
. Then, we have
h_{i}(u^{\prime})\leq h_{i}(u^{*})
for all
i\in[k]
, and the strict inequality holds for at least one
i\in[k]
. This implies that

\displaystyle h(u^{\prime})=\Gamma(h_{1}(u^{\prime}),\dots,h_{k}(u^{\prime}))<\Gamma(h_{1}(u^{*}),\dots,h_{k}(u^{*}))=h(u^{*}).

Hence,
u^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{D}^{**}
, and thus
u^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}^{**})
, which establishes (
124
).
∎

To apply Proposition
41
, the first step is to test the existence of common optimal solutions. A naive test is to solve all optimization problems (
122
) and then check the definition, which can be difficult in practice. Instead, we can consider a related multilevel optimization problem as follows. Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}\triangleq\mathcal{D}
, and for each
i=1,\dots,k
as, we define
\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{i}\triangleq\argmax_{u\in\mathcal{D}_{\!i-1}}\,h_{i}(u),\quad t_{i}\triangleq\max_{u\in\mathcal{D}_{\!i-1}}\,h_{i}(u).

(125)

Note that for each
i
, the
\mathcal{D}_{i}
solved by level
i
optimization problem gives the elements in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
that maximize
h_{i}
, and
t_{i}
denotes the corresponding optimal value. Therefore,
\mathcal{D}_{k}
can be obtained by sequentially solving
k
optimization problems as defined in (
125
).

Then, the following result provides an approach to test the existence of common optimal solutions.

Proposition 42
.

The following three statements are equivalent:

1.

The optimization problems (
122
) have common optimal solutions, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}^{*}\neq\varnothing
;

2.

t_{i}=t_{i}^{*}
for all
i=1,\dots,k
;

3.

\mathcal{D}_{i-1}\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}\neq\varnothing
for all
i=1,\dots,k
.

In addition, if one of these statements holds, then we have
\mathcal{D}_{k}=\mathcal{D}^{*}
.

Proof.

We establish the equivalence of the statements 1 to 3, by showing that “1”
\implies
“2”, “2”
\implies
“3”, and “3”
\implies
“1”.

“1”
\implies
“2”

Suppose “1” holds, and take any
u^{*}\in\mathcal{D}^{*}=\cap_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}
. We then establish “2” by induction. First, note that
u^{*}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
. For the induction step, we can show that for each
i=1,\dots,k
, if
u^{*}\in\mathcal{D}_{i-1}
, then
u^{*}\in\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
t_{i}^{*}=t_{i}
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle t_{i}^{*}\geq t_{i}=\max_{u\in\mathcal{D}_{i-1}}h_{i}(u)\geq h_{i}(u^{*})=t_{i}^{*},

where the first inequality follows from the fact that
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{0}\supset\mathcal{D}_{1}\supset\dots\supset\mathcal{D}_{k}
, where the second inequality follows from the inductive assumption
u^{*}\in\mathcal{D}_{i-1}
,
and where the last equality follows from that
u^{*}\in\mathcal{D}^{*}\subset\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}
.

“2”
\implies
“3”

For each
i=2,\dots,k
,
t_{i}=t_{i}^{*}
implies that there exists some
u_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{i-1}
, such that
h_{i}(u_{i})=t_{i}=t_{i}^{*}=\max_{u\in\mathcal{D}}h_{i}(u)
, and thus
u_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}
. Therefore,
u_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{i-1}\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}
, which establishes “3”.

“3”
\implies
“1”

For each
i=2,\dots,k
, from
\mathcal{D}_{i-1}\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}\neq\varnothing
and the definitions (
123
) and (
125
), we have
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i-1}\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}
. It can also be verified that
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{i-1}\cap\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}
holds for
i=1
. Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{k}=\mathcal{D}_{k-1}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}^{*}=\mathcal{D}_{k-2}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k-1}^{*}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}^{*}=\dots=\mathcal{D}_{0}\cap\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{D}_{i}^{*}\right)=\mathcal{D}\cap\mathcal{D}^{*}=\mathcal{D}^{*}.

(126)

This implies that
\mathcal{D}^{*}=\mathcal{D}_{k-1}\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}^{*}\neq\varnothing
.

Finally, from (
126
) we know that statement 3 implies
\mathcal{D}_{k}=\mathcal{D}^{*}
. Since all three statements are equivalent, each statement implies
\mathcal{D}_{k}=\mathcal{D}^{*}
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs

C.1
Proof of Proposition
5

Let
\hat{\gamma}\triangleq\Pi\left(\gamma;{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\right)
. We first consider the second equality of (
18
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\md_{\leq k}(\hat{\gamma})=\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma^{\prime}\colon\gamma^{\prime}=f\otimes g,\\
f\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\,k},\,g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\,k}\end{subarray}}\|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}\|

(127)

For each
k\leq\rank(\hat{\gamma})
, consider
\gamma^{\prime}=f\otimes g
with

f\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\,k},g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\,k}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}\|^{2}=\|\gamma-\hat{\gamma}+\hat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\prime}\|^{2}=\|\gamma-\hat{\gamma}\|^{2}+\|\hat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\prime}\|^{2}\geq\|\gamma-\hat{\gamma}\|^{2}+\|r_{k}(\hat{\gamma})\|^{2},

(128)

where to obtain the second equality we have used the orthogonality principle with fact that
(\gamma-\hat{\gamma})\perp\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and

(\hat{\gamma}-\gamma^{\prime})\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Note that the inequality in (
128
) holds with equality if and only if
\gamma^{\prime}=\md_{\leq k}(\hat{\gamma})
, which establishes (
127
).

We then establish
\md_{k}(\gamma|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}})=\md_{k}(\hat{\gamma})
by induction.
To begin, set
k=1
in (
127
), and the right hand side becomes
\md(\gamma|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}})
, which implies

\md(\gamma|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}})=\md(\hat{\gamma})
, i.e.,
\md_{1}(\gamma|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}})=\md_{1}(\hat{\gamma})
. As the inductive hypothesis, suppose
we have

\displaystyle\md_{i}(\gamma|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}})=\md_{i}(\hat{\gamma}),\quad i=1,\dots,m.

(129)

From (
17
), we have

\displaystyle\md_{m+1}(\gamma|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}})

\displaystyle=\md\left(\gamma-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\md_{i}(\gamma|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}})\middle|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right)

\displaystyle=\md\left(\gamma-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\md_{i}(\hat{\gamma})\middle|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right)

(130)

\displaystyle=\md\left(\Pi\left(\gamma-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\md_{i}(\hat{\gamma});{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\right)\right)

(131)

\displaystyle=\md\left(\hat{\gamma}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Pi\left(\md_{i}(\hat{\gamma});{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\right)\right)

(132)

\displaystyle=\md\left(\hat{\gamma}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\md_{i}(\hat{\gamma})\right)=\md_{m+1}(\hat{\gamma}),

(133)

where (
130
)–(
131
) follow from the inductive assumption (
129
), where (
132
) follows from the linearity of projection operator.
To obtain the first equality of
(
132
), we have again applied the assumption (
129
): for
i=1,\dots,m
,

\md_{i}(\hat{\gamma})=\md_{i}(\gamma|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}})\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Finally,
\md_{\leq k}(\gamma|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}})=\md_{\leq k}(\hat{\gamma})
can be readily obtained by definition. ∎

C.2
Proof of Proposition
7

It is easy to verify that
\corr(\hat{f}_{i}^{*},\hat{g}_{i}^{*})=\bigl\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y},\hat{f}_{i}^{*}\otimes\hat{g}_{i}^{*}\bigr\rangle=\|\md_{i}(\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}_{X;Y})\|=\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{*}
, where
\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}_{X;Y}=\Pi\left(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y};{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\right)
.

From Fact
4
, we have

(\hat{f}_{i}^{*},\hat{g}_{i}^{*})=\argmax_{(f,g)\in\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}}\,\bigl\langle\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}_{X;Y},f\otimes g\bigr\rangle
for each
i\geq 1
, where we have defined each
\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}\triangleq\{(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\times\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}\colon\|f\|=\|g\|=1\text{ and }\langle f,\hat{f}_{j}^{*}\rangle=\langle g,\hat{g}_{j}^{*}\rangle=0\text{ for all }j\in[i-1]\}.

Therefore, for each
i
and
(f,g)\in\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subset\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{i}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\corr(f_{i},g_{i})=\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y},f_{i}\otimes g_{i}\rangle=\bigl\langle\mathfrak{i}^{\prime}_{X;Y},f_{i}\otimes g_{i}\bigr\rangle\leq\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{*}=\corr(\hat{f}_{i}^{*},\hat{g}_{i}^{*}),

where the second equality follows from that
f_{i}\otimes g_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Hence, we obtain

(\hat{f}_{i}^{*},\hat{g}_{i}^{*})=\argmax_{(f,g)\in\hat{\mathcal{D}}_{i}}\corr(f,g)
. ∎

C.3
Proof of Proposition
13

The result of
\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|
directly follows from Property
1
. In addition,

\displaystyle f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(Y)=\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}(x,y)=\frac{P_{X,Y}(x,y)-P_{X}(x)P_{Y}(y)}{P_{X}(x)P_{Y}(y)},\quad\text{for all }(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},

which implies
P_{Y|X}(y|x)=P_{Y}(y)\left(1+f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(y)\right)
, i.e., (
25
).

Therefore, for all
\psi\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\,d}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\psi(Y)|X=x\right]=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y|X}(y|x)\psi(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)(1+f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(y))\psi(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)\psi(y)+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)(\psi(y)g^{\mathrm{T}}(y))f(x)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\psi(Y)\right]+\Lambda_{\psi,g}f(x),

which gives (
26
). ∎

C.4
Proof of Proposition
14

We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\psi(Y)|X=x\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y|X}(y|x)\psi(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)(1+\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}(x,y))\psi(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)[1+\md_{\leq k}(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y})](x,y)\psi(y)+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)\sum_{i>k}\sigma_{i}^{*}f_{i}^{*}(x)g_{i}^{*}(y)\psi(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)(1+f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(y))\psi(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)\psi(y)+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)(\psi(y)g^{\mathrm{T}}(y))f(x)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\psi(Y)\right]+\Lambda_{\psi,g}f(x),

where the fourth equality follows from the fact that

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)\sum_{i>k}\sigma_{i}^{*}f_{i}^{*}(x)g_{i}^{*}(y)\psi(y)=\sum_{i>k}\sigma_{i}^{*}f_{i}^{*}(x){\mathbb{E}}\left[g_{i}^{*}(Y)\psi(Y)\right]=0,

due to
\psi_{j}\in\{\mathtt{1}_{\mathcal{Y}},g_{1}^{*},\dots,g_{k}^{*}\}
for each
j\in[d]
. ∎

C.5
Proof of Property
3

The property is equivalent to
\mathcal{L}(f,g)=\mathcal{L}(f+\underline{u},g+\underline{v})
for all
\underline{u},\underline{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Hence, it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(f+\underline{u},g+\underline{v})\leq\mathcal{L}(f,g),\qquad\text{for all $\underline{u},\underline{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}$}.

(134)

Note that for all
b\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, since

\displaystyle\left(f(x)+\underline{u}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\left(g(y)+\underline{v}\right)+b(y)=f(x)\cdot g(y)+\underline{v}^{\mathrm{T}}f(x)+\underline{u}^{\mathrm{T}}g(y)+b(y)+\underline{u}^{\mathrm{T}}\underline{v},

(135)

we have [cf. (
31
)]
\tilde{P}_{Y|X}^{(f+\underline{u},g+\underline{v},b)}=\tilde{P}_{Y|X}^{(f,g,b+\underline{u}^{\mathrm{T}}g)}
,
which implies that

\mathcal{L}(f+\underline{u},g+\underline{v},b)=\mathcal{L}(f,g,b+\underline{u}^{\mathrm{T}}g)
.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(f+\underline{u},g+\underline{v})=\max_{b\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathcal{L}(f+\underline{u},g+\underline{v},b)\leq\max_{b\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathcal{L}(f,g,b+\underline{u}^{\mathrm{T}}g)\leq\mathcal{L}(f,g).

(136)

∎

C.6
Proof of Proposition
15

We first prove a useful lemma.

Lemma 43
.

Suppose
p>0,q>0
,
p+q=1
, then we have

\displaystyle\log\left(p\cdot\exp\left(\frac{u}{p}\right)+q\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{u}{q}\right)\right)\geq\min\left\{u^{2},u_{0}|u|\right\},\quad\text{for all $u\in\mathbb{R}$},

where
u_{0}\triangleq\frac{\ln 2}{3}\cdot\min\{p,q\}
.

Proof of Lemma
43
.

Let
p_{\min}\triangleq\min\{p,q\}
.

h(u)\triangleq\log\left(p\cdot\exp\left(p^{-1}u\right)+q\cdot\exp\left(-q^{-1}u\right)\right).

Then, we have

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(u)=\frac{\exp\left(p^{-1}u\right)-\exp\left(-q^{-1}u\right)}{p\cdot\exp\left(p^{-1}u\right)+q\cdot\exp\left(-q^{-1}u\right)}

\displaystyle h^{\prime\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=\left[p\cdot\exp\left(p^{-1}u\right)+q\cdot\exp\left(-q^{-1}u\right)\right]^{-2}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\left[\left(\frac{1}{p}\exp\left(\frac{u}{p}\right)+\frac{1}{q}\exp\left(-\frac{u}{q}\right)\right)\cdot\left(p\exp\left(\frac{u}{p}\right)+q\exp\left(-\frac{u}{q}\right)\right)-\left[\exp\left(\frac{u}{p}\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{u}{q}\right)\right]^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\left[\exp\left(p^{-1}u\right)+\exp\left(-q^{-1}u\right)\right]^{2}-\left[\exp\left(p^{-1}u\right)-\exp\left(-q^{-1}u\right)\right]^{2}}{\left[p\cdot\exp\left(p^{-1}u\right)+q\cdot\exp\left(-q^{-1}u\right)\right]^{2}}

\displaystyle=4\cdot\frac{\exp\left((p^{-1}-q^{-1})\cdot u\right)}{\left[p\cdot\exp\left(p^{-1}u\right)+q\cdot\exp\left(-q^{-1}u\right)\right]^{2}}.

Moreover, for all
|u|\leq u_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\exp\left((p^{-1}-q^{-1})\cdot u\right)\geq\exp\left(-|p^{-1}-q^{-1}|\cdot u_{0}\right)\geq\exp\left(-\frac{u_{0}}{p_{\min}}\right)

\displaystyle p\cdot\exp\left(p^{-1}u\right)+q\cdot\exp\left(-q^{-1}u\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{u_{0}}{p_{\min}}\right).

As a result, for all
|u|\leq u_{0}
, we have

h^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq 4\exp\left(-\frac{3u_{0}}{p_{\min}}\right)=2
.

Therefore,

h^{\prime}(u_{0})\geq h^{\prime}(0)+2\cdot(u_{0}-0)=2u_{0}>u_{0}
, and similarly,
-h^{\prime}(-u_{0})=h^{\prime}(0)-h^{\prime}(-u_{0})\geq 2u_{0}
,
i.e.,

h^{\prime}(-u_{0})\leq-2u_{0}\leq-u_{0}
.
Moreover, for all
|u|\leq u_{0}
,

h(u)\geq h(0)+h^{\prime}(0)\cdot u+\frac{1}{2}u^{2}\cdot 2=u^{2}.

Therefore, for all
u>u_{0}
,

h^{\prime}(u)\geq h^{\prime}(u_{0})>u_{0}
,
which implies that

h(u)\geq h(u_{0})+u_{0}(u-u_{0})\geq u_{0}^{2}+u_{0}u-u_{0}^{2}=u_{0}u
.
Similarly, we have
h(u)\geq-u_{0}u
for all
u<-u_{0}
.
∎

Proceeding to the proof of Proposition
15
, we consider zero-mean
k
-dimensional
f
,
g
. Without loss of generality, we assume
b\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}
satisfies
{\mathbb{E}}\left[b(Y)\right]=-H(Y)
. Then, let
a\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
be
a(y)\triangleq b(y)-\log P_{Y}(y)
, and define

\gamma\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}
as
\gamma(x,y)\triangleq f(x)\cdot g(y)+a(y)
.
Note that since

\exp(f(x)\cdot g(y)+b(y))=P_{Y}(y)\exp(f(x)\cdot g(y)+a(y))=P_{Y}(y)\exp(\gamma(x,y)),

we have

\displaystyle\tilde{P}^{(f,g,b)}_{Y|X}(y|x)=\frac{\exp(f(x)\cdot g(y)+b(y))}{\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}\exp(f(x)\cdot g(y^{\prime})+b(y^{\prime}))}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{Y}(y)\exp(\gamma(x,y))}{\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(x,y^{\prime}))}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(f,g,b)

\displaystyle\quad={\mathbb{E}}_{(\hat{X},{\hat{Y}})\sim P_{X}P_{Y}}\left[(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}(\hat{X},{\hat{Y}})+1)\cdot\log\tilde{P}^{(f,g,b)}_{Y|X}({\hat{Y}}|\hat{X})\right]

\displaystyle\quad={\mathbb{E}}_{(\hat{X},{\hat{Y}})\sim P_{X}P_{Y}}\left[(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}(\hat{X},{\hat{Y}})+1)\cdot\left(\log P_{Y}({\hat{Y}})+\gamma(\hat{X},{\hat{Y}})-\log\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(\hat{X},y^{\prime}))\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad=-H(Y)+\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y},\gamma\rangle-{\mathbb{E}}_{\hat{X}\sim P_{X}}\left[\log\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(\hat{X},y^{\prime}))\right].

(137)

As a result, for all
(f,g,b)
that satisfies

\mathcal{L}(f,g,b)\geq-H(Y)
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y},\gamma\rangle\geq{\mathbb{E}}_{\hat{X}\sim P_{X}}\left[\log\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(\hat{X},y^{\prime}))\right].

(138)

In addition, note that for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(x,y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=P_{Y}(y)\exp(\gamma(x,y))+(1-P_{Y}(y))\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}\setminus\{y\}}\frac{P_{Y}(y^{\prime})}{1-P_{Y}(y)}\exp(\gamma(x,y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\geq P_{Y}(y)\exp(\gamma(x,y))+(1-P_{Y}(y))\exp\left(-\frac{P_{Y}(y)}{1-P_{Y}(y)}\cdot\gamma(x,y)\right).

where the inequality follows from Jensen’s inequality and
\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)\gamma(x,y)=0
.

Let us define

\displaystyle q_{X}\triangleq\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P_{X}(x)>0,\quad q_{Y}\triangleq\min_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y)>0.

Then, from Lemma
43
we have

\displaystyle\log\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(x,y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\geq\log\left[P_{Y}(y)\exp(\gamma(x,y))+(1-P_{Y}(y))\exp\left(-\frac{P_{Y}(y)}{1-P_{Y}(y)}\cdot\gamma(x,y)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\min\left\{(P_{Y}(y)\gamma(x,y))^{2},\frac{\ln 2}{3}\cdot q_{Y}|P_{Y}(y)\gamma(x,y)|\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\ln 2\cdot q_{Y}^{2}}{3}\cdot\min\left\{(\gamma(x,y))^{2},|\gamma(x,y)|\right\},

which implies that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\hat{X}\sim P_{X}}\left[\log\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(\hat{X},y^{\prime}))\right]

\displaystyle\geq P_{X}(x)\cdot\log\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(x,y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\ln 2\cdot q_{X}q_{Y}^{2}}{3}\cdot\min\left\{(\gamma(x,y))^{2},|\gamma(x,y)|\right\}.

(139)

On the other hand, since
\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|=O(\epsilon)
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|\leq C\epsilon
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y},\gamma\rangle\leq\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|\cdot\|\gamma\|\leq\gamma_{\max}\cdot\epsilon,

(140)

where
\gamma_{\max}\triangleq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y}}|\gamma(x,y)|
.

Hence, by combining (
138
), (
139
) and (
140
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\ln 2\cdot q_{X}q_{Y}^{2}}{3}\cdot\min\left\{\gamma_{\max}^{2},\gamma_{\max}\right\}\leq C\cdot\gamma_{\max}\cdot\epsilon,

(141)

where we have taken
(x,y)=\argmax_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}|\gamma(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|
in (
139
).

From (
141
), if
\epsilon<\frac{\ln 2}{3}\cdot\frac{q_{X}q_{Y}^{2}}{3C}

we have
\gamma_{\max}<\epsilon
. This implies that

\displaystyle|\gamma(x,y)|<\epsilon,\quad\text{for all }x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y},

(142)

and we obtain
\|\gamma\|<\epsilon
. In addition, note that since

\|\gamma\|^{2}=\|f\otimes g+a\|^{2}=\|f\otimes g\|^{2}+\|a\|^{2}
,
we obtain
\|f\otimes g\|=O(\epsilon)
.

From (
142
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(x,y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\left(1+\gamma(x,y^{\prime})+\frac{(\gamma(x,y^{\prime}))^{2}}{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})(\gamma(x,y^{\prime}))^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{2}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{{\hat{Y}}\sim P_{Y}}\left[\gamma(x,{\hat{Y}})\right]^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\hat{X}\sim P_{X}}\left[\log\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(\hat{X},y^{\prime}))\right]=\frac{1}{2}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{(\hat{X},{\hat{Y}})\sim P_{X}P_{Y}}\left[\gamma(\hat{X},{\hat{Y}})\right]^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\gamma\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2}),

and the likelihood (
137
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(f,g,b)

\displaystyle=-H(Y)+\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y},\gamma\rangle-{\mathbb{E}}_{\hat{X}\sim P_{X}}\left[\log\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{Y}(y^{\prime})\exp(\gamma(\hat{X},y^{\prime}))\right]

\displaystyle=-H(Y)+\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y},\gamma\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\gamma\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|^{2}-\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-\gamma\|^{2}\right)-H(Y)+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|^{2}-\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-f\otimes g-a\|^{2}\right)-H(Y)+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|^{2}-\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-f\otimes g\|^{2}-\|a\|^{2}\right)-H(Y)+o(\epsilon^{2}),

(143)

where the last equality follows from the fact that

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-f\otimes g-a\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-f\otimes g\|^{2}+\|a\|^{2},

due to the orthogonality
(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-f\otimes g)\perp\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}\ni a
.

Finally, from (
143
), for given
f,g
,
\mathcal{L}(f,g,b)
is maximized when
\|a\|=o(\epsilon)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(f,g)=\max_{b\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathcal{L}(f,g,b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|^{2}-\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-f\otimes g\|^{2}\right)-H(Y)+o(\epsilon^{2}),

which gives (
33
). ∎

C.7
Proof of Theorem
20

Throughout this proof, we consider

\displaystyle L_{1}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\triangleq\bigl\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}\bigr\|^{2},\quad L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\triangleq\bigl\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}-f\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}

defined on the domain

\mathcal{D}\triangleq\{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\colon\bar{f}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\,{\bar{k}}},\bar{g}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\,{\bar{k}}},f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\,k},g\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\,k}\}
,
and let

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}^{*}\triangleq\argmin_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}}L_{1}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g),\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}^{*}\triangleq\argmin_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}}L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g).

We also define
\bar{\gamma},\gamma\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}
, as

\displaystyle\bar{\gamma}\triangleq\Pi\left(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y};{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\right),\quad\gamma\triangleq\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-\bar{\gamma}=\Pi\left(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y};{(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbin{\boxminus}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}})\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\right).

(144)

Then, it suffices to establish that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{*}\triangleq\mathcal{D}_{1}^{*}\cap\mathcal{D}_{2}^{*}=\{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}\colon\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}=\bar{\gamma},f\otimes g

\displaystyle=\md_{\leq k}(\gamma)\}.

(145)

To see this, note that the common solution
\mathcal{D}^{*}
(
145
) coincides with the optimal solution (
47
) to be established. From Proposition
41
, since
\mathcal{D}^{*}\neq\varnothing
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{*}=\argmin_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}}L_{1}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)+L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g).

(146)

Furthermore, from the definition of the H-score (cf. Definition
11
), we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bar{f}\,\\
f\,\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bar{g}\\
g\end{bmatrix};\mathcal{C}_{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{H}(\bar{f},\bar{g})+\mathscr{H}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bar{f}\,\\
f\,\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bar{g}\\
g\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|^{2}-L_{1}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|^{2}-L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\right)

\displaystyle=\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(L_{1}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)+L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\right),

which implies that

\displaystyle\argmax_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}}\mathscr{H}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bar{f}\,\\
f\,\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bar{g}\\
g\end{bmatrix};\mathcal{C}_{\pi}\right)=\argmin_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}}L_{1}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)+L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)=\mathcal{D}^{*}.

It remains only to establish (
145
). From Proposition
42
, it suffices to establish that
t_{2}=t_{2}^{*}
and (cf. (
145
))

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{2}=\{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}\colon\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}=\bar{\gamma},f\otimes g=\gamma\},

(147)

where we have defined

\displaystyle t_{2}^{*}\triangleq\min_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}}L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g),\quad t_{2}\triangleq\min_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{*}}L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g),\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}\triangleq\argmin_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{*}}L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g).

To this end, let us define
\bar{h}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\,k}
and
\bar{h}\in(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbin{\boxminus}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}})^{k}
as
\bar{h}\triangleq\Pi\left(f;{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}}\right)
, and
h\triangleq f-\bar{h}=\Pi\left(f;{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbin{\boxminus}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}}\right)
. Then, we have

\displaystyle L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}-f\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}

(148)

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\bar{\gamma}+\gamma-\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}-(\bar{h}+h)\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}

(149)

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\bar{\gamma}-\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}-\bar{h}\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}+\bigl\|\gamma-h\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}

(150)

\displaystyle\geq\bigl\|\gamma-h\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}

(151)

\displaystyle\geq\bigl\|r_{k}(\gamma)\bigr\|^{2}

(152)

where (
150
) follows from the orthogonality principle, since

\left(\bar{\gamma}-\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}-\bar{h}\otimes g\right)\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
\left(\gamma-h\otimes g\right)\perp\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Moreover, it is easily verified that the lower bound (
152
) is tight: all inequalities hold with equality for
(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)
satisfying (
47
).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle t_{2}^{*}=\min_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}}L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)=\bigl\|r_{k}(\gamma)\bigr\|^{2}

(153)

On the other hand, since
{\bar{k}}\geq\rank(\bar{\gamma})
, from Proposition
5
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{*}=\{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\colon\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}=\bar{\gamma}\}
. Hence, for all
(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)=\bigl\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}-f\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}=\bigl\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}-\bar{\gamma}-f\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}=\bigl\|\gamma-f\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}\geq\bigl\|r_{k}(\gamma)\bigr\|^{2},

where the inequality holds with equality if and only if
f\otimes g=\md_{\leq k}(\gamma)
.

As a result,
\mathcal{D}_{2}=\argmin_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{*}}L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)
is given by (
147
), and we have

\displaystyle t_{2}=\min_{(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{*}}L_{2}(\bar{f},\bar{g},f,g)=\bigl\|r_{k}(\gamma)\bigr\|^{2}=t_{2}^{*}.

∎

C.8
Proof of Theorem
21

To begin, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bar{f}\,\\
f\,\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bar{g}\\
g\end{bmatrix};\mathcal{C}_{\pi}^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{{\bar{k}}}\mathscr{H}(\bar{f}_{[i]},\bar{g}_{[i]})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathscr{H}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bar{f}\\
f_{[i]}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bar{g}\\
g_{[i]}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{{\bar{k}}-1}\mathscr{H}(\bar{f}_{[i]},\bar{g}_{[i]})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(k^{-1}\cdot\mathscr{H}(\bar{f},\bar{g})+\mathscr{H}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bar{f}\\
f_{[i]}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bar{g}\\
g_{[i]}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right).

Note that for
\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bar{f}\,\\
f\,\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bar{g}\\
g\end{bmatrix}\right)\in\dom(\mathcal{C}_{\pi}^{\star})
and each
i\in[{\bar{k}}]
,
\mathscr{H}(\bar{f}_{[i]},\bar{g}_{[i]})
is maximized if and only if

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{[i]}\otimes\bar{g}_{[i]}=\md_{\leq i}(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y}|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}).

(154)

In addition, from Theorem
20
, for each
i\in[k]
, the term

\left(k^{-1}\cdot\mathscr{H}(\bar{f},\bar{g})+\mathscr{H}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bar{f}\\
f_{[i]}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bar{g}\\
g_{[i]}\end{bmatrix}\right)\right)

is maximized if and only if

\displaystyle\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}=\Pi\left(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y};{\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\right),\qquad f_{[i]}\otimes g_{[i]}=\md_{\leq i}(\Pi\left(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y};{(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}\mathbin{\boxminus}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}})\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\right)).

(155)

Note that
(
48
) is the common solution of (
154
) and (
155
). Hence,
the proof is completed by applying Proposition
41
.
∎

C.9
Proof of Proposition
23

The relation
\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}=\tilde{\ell}_{P^{\mathsf{M}}_{X,S,Y}}
can be directly verified from definition. To establish
\pi_{\mathsf{M}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X;S,Y})=\Pi\left(\mathfrak{i}_{X;S,Y};{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{S}}}\right)=\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}
,
from Fact
2
, it suffices to show that
(\mathfrak{i}_{X;S,Y}-\mathfrak{i}_{X;S})\perp\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{S}}
.

To this end, note that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{X;S,Y}(x,s,y)-\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}(x,s)

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)-P^{\mathsf{M}}_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)}{R_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)-P_{X|S}(x|s)P_{S}(s)P_{Y|S}(y|s)}{R_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)}.

Therefore, for all
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{S}}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;S,Y}-\mathfrak{i}_{X;S},f\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S},y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)f(x,s)-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S},y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{X|S}(x|s)P_{S}(s)P_{Y|S}(y|s)\cdot f(x,s)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{P_{X,S}}\left[f(X,S)\right]-{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{X,S}}\left[f(X,S)\right]

\displaystyle=0.

∎

C.10
Proof of Proposition
24

Let
\gamma=\mathfrak{i}_{X;S,Y}^{(Q)}
, then the statement is equivalent to

\displaystyle\gamma\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{S}}\quad\iff\quad Q_{X,S,Y}=Q_{X|S}Q_{S}Q_{Y|S}.

“
\implies
”

If
\gamma\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{S}}
, then we have

\displaystyle Q_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)

\displaystyle=Q_{X}(x)Q_{S,Y}(s,y)\left(1+\gamma(x,s)\right)

\displaystyle=P_{X}(x)P_{S,Y}(s,y)\left(1+\gamma(x,s)\right)

\displaystyle=P_{X}(x)P_{S}(s)\left(1+\gamma(x,s)\right)\cdot P_{Y|S}(y|s),\quad\text{for all $x,s,y$}.

(156)

Therefore,

\displaystyle Q_{X,S}(x,s)=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}Q_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)=P_{X}(x)P_{S}(s)\left(1+\gamma(x,s)\right).

(157)

From (
156
) and (
157
), we obtain

\displaystyle Q_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)=Q_{X,S}(x,s)P_{Y|S}(y|s)=Q_{X,S}(x,s)Q_{Y|S}(y|s)=Q_{X|S}(x|s)Q_{S}(s)Q_{Y|S}(y|s).

(158)

“
\impliedby
”

It suffices to note that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{X;S,Y}^{(Q)}(x,s,y)=\frac{Q_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)}{Q_{X}(x)Q_{S,Y}(s,y)}-1=\frac{Q_{X,S}(x,s)Q_{Y|S}(y|s)}{Q_{X}(x)Q_{S}(s)Q_{Y|S}(y|s)}-1=\frac{Q_{X,S}(x,s)}{Q_{X}(x)Q_{S}(s)}-1.

∎

C.11
Proof of Proposition
26

The results on
\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}\|
and
\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}\|
directly follows from Property
1
. To establish (
63
), note that from Proposition
23
, we have

\displaystyle P_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)-P_{X|S}(x|s)P_{S}(s)P_{Y|S}(y|s)=P_{X}(x)P_{S,Y}(s,y)\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y),

which implies that

\displaystyle P_{Y|X,S}(y|x,s)

\displaystyle=P_{Y|S}(y|s)\cdot\left(1+\frac{P_{X}(x)P_{S}(s)}{P_{X,S}(x,s)}\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y)\right)

\displaystyle=P_{Y|S}(y|s)\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{1+\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}(x,s)}\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y)\right)

(159)

\displaystyle=P_{Y|S}(y|s)\cdot\left(1+\frac{f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(s,y)}{1+\bar{f}^{\,\mathrm{T}}(x)\bar{g}(s)}\right),

(160)

which further implies
(
64
). ∎

C.12
Proof of Proposition
27

When
X
and
(S,Y)
are
\epsilon
-dependent, we have
\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;S,Y}\|=O(\epsilon)
, and thus

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}(x,s)=O(\epsilon),\quad\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y)=O(\epsilon).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}(x,s)}\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y)=\left(1-\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}(x,s)\right)\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y)+o(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y)+o(\epsilon).

Then, it follows from (
159
) that

\displaystyle P_{Y|X,S}(y|x,s)=P_{Y|S}(y|s)\cdot\left(1+\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y)\right)+o(\epsilon).

Finally, the proof is completed by using the decomposition (
66
). ∎

C.13
Proof of Theorem
28

For each
s,x,y
, let us define
R_{X,Y}^{(s)}(x,y)\triangleq P_{X|S=s}(x)P_{Y|S=s}(y)
and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{(s)}_{X;Y}(x,y)\triangleq\frac{P_{X,Y|S=s}(x,y)-P_{X|S=s}(x)P_{Y|S=s}(y)}{P_{X|S=s}(x)P_{Y|S=s}(y)}.

(161)

In addition, we define
\underline{\mu}_{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
as
\underline{\mu}_{s}\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\left[f(X)|S=s\right]
for each
s\in\mathcal{S}
. Also, let
\beta\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}}
be defined as
\beta(s,y)\triangleq\underline{\mu}_{s}^{\mathrm{T}}g(s,y)
.

Then, for each
s\in\mathcal{S}
, from
(
72
) and Proposition
15
we have

\displaystyle|f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(s,y)-\beta(s,y)|=|(f(x)-\underline{\mu}_{s})^{\mathrm{T}}g(s,y)|=O(\epsilon),\quad\text{for all $x,s,y$},

(162)

which implies that

\displaystyle\left|f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(s,y)\right|=O(\epsilon),\quad\text{for all $x,s,y$}.

(163)

In addition, from Property
3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{S}^{(s)}(f,g^{(s)})

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathcal{L}_{S}^{(s)}(f-\underline{\mu}_{s},g^{(s)})

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\bigl\|\mathfrak{i}^{(s)}_{X;Y}\bigr\|_{R_{X,Y}^{(s)}}^{2}-\bigl\|\mathfrak{i}^{(s)}_{X;Y}-(f-\underline{\mu}_{s})\otimes g^{(s)}\bigr\|_{R_{X,Y}^{(s)}}^{2}\right)-H(Y|S=s)+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigl\langle\mathfrak{i}^{(s)}_{X;Y},f\otimes g^{(s)}-\underline{\mu}_{s}^{\mathrm{T}}g^{(s)}\bigr\rangle_{R_{X,Y}^{(s)}}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\bigl\|f\otimes g^{(s)}-\underline{\mu}_{s}^{\mathrm{T}}g^{(s)}\bigr\|_{R_{X,Y}^{(s)}}^{2}-H(Y|S=s)+o(\epsilon^{2}),

(164)

where
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{R}
and
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{R}
denote the inner product and corresponding induced norm on the function space, with respect to the metric distribution
R
.

For the first two terms in (
164
), we compute their expectations over
P_{S}
. For the first term,

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}P_{S}(s)\bigl\langle\mathfrak{i}^{(s)}_{X;Y},f\otimes g^{(s)}-\underline{\mu}_{s}^{\mathrm{T}}g^{(s)}\bigr\rangle_{R_{X,Y}^{(s)}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S},y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{S}(s)R_{X,Y}^{(s)}(x,y)\mathfrak{i}^{(s)}_{X;Y}(x,y)\left(f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g^{(s)}(y)-\underline{\mu}_{s}^{\mathrm{T}}g^{(s)}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S},y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{X}(x)P_{S,Y}(s,y)\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y)\cdot\left(f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(s,y)-\beta(s,y)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigl\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S},f\otimes g\bigr\rangle-\bigl\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S},\beta\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigl\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S},f\otimes g\bigr\rangle

(165)

where to obtain the second equality we have used the facts that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}^{(s)}_{X;Y}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{X,Y|S=s}(x,y)-P_{X|S=s}(x)P_{Y|S=s}(y)}{P_{X|S=s}(x)P_{Y|S=s}(y)}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)-P^{\mathsf{M}}_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)}{P_{X}(x)P_{S,Y}(s,y)}\cdot\frac{P_{X}(x)}{P_{X|S=s}(x)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}(x,s,y)\cdot\frac{1}{1+\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}(x,s)}

(166)

and

\displaystyle P_{S}(s)R_{X,Y}^{(s)}(x,y)=P^{\mathsf{M}}_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)

\displaystyle=P_{X}(x)P_{S,Y}(s,y)\cdot(1+\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}(x,s)),

(167)

and where to obtain (
165
) we have used the fact that
\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}\perp\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}}\ni\beta
.

For the second term of (
164
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}P_{S}(s)\bigl\|f\otimes g^{(s)}-\underline{\mu}_{s}^{\mathrm{T}}g^{(s)}\bigr\|_{R_{X,Y}^{(s)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S},y\in\mathcal{Y}}P^{\mathsf{M}}_{X,S,Y}(x,s,y)\cdot\left[(f(x)-\underline{\mu}_{s})^{\mathrm{T}}g(s,y)\right]^{2}

(168)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S},y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{X}(x)P_{S,Y}(s,y)\cdot\left[(f(x)-\underline{\mu}_{s})^{\mathrm{T}}g(s,y)\right]^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})

(169)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X},s\in\mathcal{S},y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{X}(x)P_{S,Y}(s,y)\cdot\left[f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(s,y)-\beta(s,y)\right]^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})

(170)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\|f\otimes g-\beta\right\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})

(171)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\|f\otimes g\right\|^{2}+\left\|\beta\right\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2}),

(172)

where to obtain the second equality we have used
(
162
), (
167
), and the fact that
\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}\|=O(\epsilon)
.
Furthermore, we can show that
\|\beta\|=o(\epsilon)
. To see this, note that

\displaystyle\beta(s,y)=\underline{\mu}_{s}^{\mathrm{T}}g(s,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P_{X|S}(x|s)f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(s,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P_{X}(x)\cdot[1+\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}(x,s)]\cdot f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(s,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P_{X}(x)\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}(x,s)\cdot f^{\mathrm{T}}(x)g(s,y).

Then, from
\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;S}\|=O(\epsilon)
and (
163
), we obtain
|\beta(s,y)|=O(\epsilon^{2})
and
\|\beta\|=O(\epsilon^{2})=o(\epsilon)
.

Therefore, we can refine (
172
) as

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}P_{S}(s)\bigl\|f\otimes g^{(s)}-\underline{\mu}_{s}^{\mathrm{T}}g^{(s)}\bigr\|_{R_{X,Y}^{(s)}}^{2}=\left\|f\otimes g\right\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2}).

(173)

Combining
(
164
),
(
165
), and (
173
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{S}(f,g)=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}P_{S}(s)\cdot\mathcal{L}_{S}^{(s)}(f,g^{(s)})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S},f\otimes g\right\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\bigl\|f\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}-H(Y|S)+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}\|^{2}-\bigl\|\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S}-f\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}\right)-H(Y|S)+o(\epsilon^{2}),

which is maximized if and only if
f\otimes g=\md_{\leq k}(\mathfrak{i}_{X;Y|S})+o(\epsilon)
.
∎

C.14
Proof of Proposition
29

Given any
h\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{Y}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}}
, we can represent
h=h^{(1)}+h^{(2)}
with
h^{(i)}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{i}\times\mathcal{Y}},i=1,2
, i.e.,
h(x_{1},x_{2},y)=h^{(1)}(x_{1},y)+h^{(2)}(x_{2},y)
.

Then, for any
Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{B}}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y},h^{(i)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2},y\in\mathcal{Y}}P_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})P_{Y}(y)\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}(x_{1},x_{2},y)\cdot h^{(i)}(x_{i},y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2},y\in\mathcal{Y}}[Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}(x_{1},x_{2},y)-P_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})P_{Y}(y)]\cdot h^{(i)}(x_{i},y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}_{i},y\in\mathcal{Y}}[P_{X_{i},Y}(x_{i},y)-P_{X_{i}}(x_{i})P_{Y}(y)]\cdot h^{(i)}(x_{i},y)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X_{i};Y},h^{(i)}\rangle\qquad\qquad\text{for $i=1,2$.}

As a result,

\displaystyle\langle\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),h\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}-\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),h\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y},h\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y},h^{(1)}\rangle+\langle\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y},h^{(2)}\rangle\equiv\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1};Y},h^{(1)}\rangle+\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X_{2};Y},h^{(2)}\rangle,

where the second equality follows from the fact that

\langle\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),h\rangle=0
.

Hence, we obtain

\langle\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})-\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\tilde{\ell}_{P_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}),h\rangle=0

for all
h\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{Y}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}}
, which implies that
\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}^{(Q)}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})-\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\tilde{\ell}_{P_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})=0
. ∎

C.15
Proof of Proposition
31

From the definition, we have

\displaystyle P_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}(x_{1},x_{2},y)

\displaystyle=P_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})P_{Y}(y)\left(1+[\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})](x_{1},x_{2},y)+[\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})](x_{1},x_{2},y)\right)

\displaystyle=P_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})P_{Y}(y)\left[1+\bar{f}^{\,\mathrm{T}}(x_{1},x_{2})\bar{g}(y)+f^{\mathrm{T}}(x_{1},x_{2})g(y)\right],

which implies (
83a
).

To obtain (
83b
), note that from (
174
), we have

\displaystyle P_{X_{1},Y}(x_{1},y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}}P^{\sf B}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}(x_{1},x_{2},y)

\displaystyle=P_{X_{1}}(x_{1})P_{Y}(y)\left[1+{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bar{f}^{\,\mathrm{T}}(x_{1},X_{2})\bar{g}(y)|X_{1}=x_{1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=P_{X_{1}}(x_{1})P_{Y}(y)\left[1+\left(\bar{f}^{(1)}(x_{1})+[\tau_{{1}}(\bar{f}^{(2)})](x_{1})\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\bar{g}(y)\right],

where to obtain the last equality we have used the fact that

\tau_{{1}}(\bar{f})=\bar{f}^{(1)}+\tau_{{1}}(\bar{f}^{(2)})
.
Similarly, we can obtain (
83b
).

Finally, (
84
) can be readily obtained from (
83
). ∎

C.16
Proof of Proposition
32

Note that since
\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}}
, we have

\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}}
, which implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{(x_{1},x_{2},y)\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}}P_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})P_{Y}(y)\cdot[\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})](x_{1},x_{2},y)=\langle\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),1\rangle=0.

Therefore, it follows from the definition of
P^{\sf B}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
that
\displaystyle\sum_{(x_{1},x_{2},y)\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}}P^{\sf B}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}(x_{1},x_{2},y)=1
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{(x_{1},x_{2},y)\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}}P^{\sf I}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}(x_{1},x_{2},y)=1
.

Moreover, from (
85
), we have

P^{\sf B}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}(x_{1},x_{2},y)\geq 0,P^{\sf I}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}(x_{1},x_{2},y)\geq 0
for all
(x_{1},x_{2},y)
. Therefore,
we obtain
P^{\sf B}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y},P^{\sf I}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}}
.

Since
\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}\perp\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}}
, we have
\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})\perp\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}}
. Therefore, for any
(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}
, let
f(x_{1},x_{2})=\delta_{x_{1}\hat{x}_{1}}\delta_{x_{2}\hat{x}_{2}}
, then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}[\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})](\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(x_{1},x_{2},y)\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}}[\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})](x_{1},x_{2},y)\cdot\delta_{x_{1}\hat{x}_{1}}\delta_{x_{2}\hat{x}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),f\rangle=0.

This implies that
P^{\sf B}_{X_{1},X_{2}}=P_{X_{1},X_{2}}
. Similarly, we have
P^{\sf I}_{X_{1},X_{2}}=P_{X_{1},X_{2}}
.

Finally, note that since

\displaystyle P_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}(x_{1},x_{2},y)=P_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})P_{Y}(y)\left[1+[\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})](x_{1},x_{2},y)+[\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})](x_{1},x_{2},y)\right],

we have

\displaystyle P_{X_{1},Y}(x_{1},y)-P^{\sf B}_{X_{1},Y}(x_{1},y)

\displaystyle=P^{\sf I}_{X_{1},Y}(x_{1},y)-P_{X_{1}}(x_{1})P_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}}P_{X_{1},X_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime})P_{Y}(y)[\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})](x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle=0.

To obtain the last equality, note that for any
\hat{x}_{1},{\hat{y}}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{Y}
, let us define
\gamma\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{Y}}
as
\gamma(x_{1},y)=\delta_{x_{1}\hat{x}_{1}}\delta_{y{\hat{y}}}
. Then, from
\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})\perp\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{Y}}
, we have

\displaystyle 0=\langle\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),\gamma\rangle=\sum_{x_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}}P_{X_{1},X_{2}}(\hat{x}_{1},x_{2}^{\prime})P_{Y}({\hat{y}})[\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})](\hat{x}_{1},x_{2}^{\prime},{\hat{y}}).

(174)

Similarly, we can show that

\displaystyle P_{X_{2},Y}(x_{2},y)-P^{\sf B}_{X_{2},Y}(x_{2},y)

\displaystyle=P^{\sf I}_{X_{2},Y}(x_{2},y)-P_{X_{2}}(x_{2})P_{Y}(y)=0.

∎

C.17
Proof of Proposition
33

Note that since

\displaystyle H(Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})

\displaystyle=H(P_{X_{1},X_{2}})+H(P_{Y})-I_{Q}(X_{1},X_{2};Y),

we have

\displaystyle P^{{\mathsf{ent}}}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}=\argmin_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathsf{B}}}I_{Q}(X_{1},X_{2};Y),

(175)

where
I_{Q}(X_{1},X_{2};Y)
denotes the mutual information between
(X_{1},X_{2})
and
Y
with respect to
Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
.

Specifically, when we take

P_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
as the
Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
, we have

\displaystyle I_{P}(X_{1},X_{2};Y)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2}).

Therefore, to solve (
175
), it suffices to consider
Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
with
I_{Q}(X_{1},X_{2};Y)<I_{P}(X_{1},X_{2};Y)=O(\epsilon^{2})
. Since
Q_{X_{1},X_{2}}Q_{Y}=P_{X_{1},X_{2}}P_{Y}\in\relint(\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}})
, we have
\|\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\|^{2}=O(\epsilon^{2})
[cf.
(
Sason and Verdú 2016
, Eq. (338))
].

Therefore,

\displaystyle I_{Q}(X_{1},X_{2};Y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2})

where the inequality holds with equality when
\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})\bigr\|=o(\epsilon)
. Hence, we obtain

\mathfrak{i}^{({\mathsf{ent}})}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}=\tilde{\ell}_{P^{{\mathsf{ent}}}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}=\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})+o(\epsilon)
,
which gives (
88
). ∎

C.18
Proof of Proposition
34

For all
\phi\triangleq\phi^{(1)}+\phi^{(2)}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}
with
\|\psi\|=1
, we have
{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{X_{1},X_{2}}P_{Y}}\left[\psi(Y)\phi(X_{1},X_{2})\right]=0
. Hence,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\psi(Y)\phi(X_{1},X_{2})\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{P_{X_{1},X_{2}}P_{Y}}\left[(1+\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}(X_{1},X_{2},Y))\cdot\psi(Y)\phi(X_{1},X_{2})\right]

\displaystyle=\langle\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y},\phi\otimes\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),\phi\otimes\psi\rangle+\langle\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),\phi\otimes\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),\phi\otimes\psi\rangle,

where the first equality follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}
, and where
the last equality follow from the fact that
\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})\perp(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{Y}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}})\ni\phi\otimes\psi
.

Therefore, we can rewrite the objective (
92
) as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\psi(Y)-\phi^{(1)}(X_{1})-\phi^{(2)}(X_{2})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\psi(Y)-\phi(X_{1},X_{2})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\|\psi\|^{2}+\|\phi\|^{2}-2\cdot{\mathbb{E}}\left[\psi(Y)\phi(X_{1},X_{2})\right]

\displaystyle=1+\|\phi\otimes\psi\|^{2}-2\langle\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}),\phi\otimes\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=1+\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})-\phi\otimes\psi\|^{2}-\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})\|^{2}.

Finally, the proof is completed by noting that
\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{{\bar{K}}}\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}
, and we have

\displaystyle\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y})-\phi\otimes\psi\|^{2}\geq\|{r_{1}(\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}))}\|^{2}=\sum_{i=2}^{{\bar{K}}}\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2},

where the inequality becomes equality if and only if
\phi\otimes\psi=\md(\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathfrak{i}_{X_{1},X_{2};Y}))=\bar{\sigma}_{1}(\bar{f}_{1}^{*}\otimes\bar{g}_{1}^{*})
.

∎

C.19
Proof of Theorem
35

We first introduce a useful lemma.

Lemma 44
.

For all
k\geq 1,f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\,k},g\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\,k}
, let
\bar{h}\triangleq\Pi\left(f;{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}}\right)
,
h=f-\bar{h}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\rm{m}}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left[L(R_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})-\eta_{0}\cdot\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-h\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}-L_{\sf B}(\bar{h}\otimes g)\right],

(176)

where we have defined

\displaystyle L_{\sf B}(\gamma)\triangleq\eta_{0}\cdot\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\gamma)\bigr\|^{2}+\eta_{1}\cdot\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(1)}_{X_{1},Y}}-\pi_{\mathsf{M}_{1}}(\gamma)\bigr\|^{2}+\eta_{2}\cdot\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(2)}_{X_{2},Y}}-\pi_{\mathsf{M}_{2}}(\gamma)\bigr\|^{2}.

(177)

Proof.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(f,g;\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}-\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}-f\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}-\bigl\|(\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-h\otimes g)+(\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-\bar{h}\otimes g)\bigr\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}-\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-h\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}-\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-\bar{h}\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}\right),

(178)

where to obtain the last equality we have used the orthogonality between

\left(\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-h\otimes g\right)\perp(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{Y}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}})
and

\left(\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-\bar{h}\otimes g\right)\in(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{Y}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{Y}})
.

In addition, for each
i=1,2
, from

\tau_{{i}}(f)=\tau_{{i}}(\bar{h})
we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(\tau_{{i}}(f),g;\hat{P}^{(i)}_{X_{i},Y})=\mathscr{H}(\tau_{{i}}(\bar{h}),g;\hat{P}^{(i)}_{X_{i},Y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(i)}_{X_{i},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}-\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(i)}_{X_{i},Y}}-\tau_{{i}}(\bar{h})\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}\right),

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(i)}_{X_{i},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}-\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(i)}_{X_{i},Y}}-\pi_{\mathsf{M}_{i}}(\bar{h}\otimes g)\bigr\|^{2}\right),

(179)

where the last equality follows from that
\pi_{\mathsf{M}_{i}}(\bar{h}\otimes g)=\tau_{{i}}(\bar{h})\otimes g
.

From (
178
) and (
179
), we can rewrite (
95
) as

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\rm{m}}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\eta_{0}\cdot\mathscr{H}(f,g;\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})+\eta_{1}\cdot\mathscr{H}\left(\tau_{{1}}(f),g;\hat{P}^{(1)}_{X_{1},Y}\right)+\eta_{2}\cdot\mathscr{H}\left(\tau_{{2}}(f),g;\hat{P}^{(2)}_{X_{2},Y}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{0}}{2}\cdot\left(\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}-\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-h\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}-\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-\bar{h}\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{\eta_{i}}{2}\cdot\left(\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(i)}_{X_{i},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}-\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(i)}_{X_{1},Y}}-\pi_{\mathsf{M}_{i}}(\bar{h}\otimes g)\bigr\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left[L(R_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})-\eta_{0}\cdot\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-h\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\left[\eta_{0}\cdot\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-\bar{h}\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\eta_{i}\cdot\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(i)}_{X_{1},Y}}-\pi_{\mathsf{M}_{i}}(\bar{h}\otimes g)\bigr\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left[L(R_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})-\eta_{0}\cdot\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-h\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}-L_{\sf B}(\bar{h}\otimes g)\right],

where the last equality follows from the fact that

\bar{h}\otimes g=\pi_{\mathsf{B}}(\bar{h}\otimes g)
.

∎

Let use define
\bar{h}\triangleq\Pi\left(f;{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}}\right)
and
h\triangleq f-\bar{h}
. Then, from Lemma
44
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\rm{m}}(\bar{f},\bar{g})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left[L(R_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})-\eta_{0}\cdot\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})\bigr\|^{2}-L_{\sf B}(\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g})\right],

(180)

and, similarly,

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\rm{m}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bar{f}\,\\
f\,\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bar{g}\\
g\end{bmatrix}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left[L(R_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})-\eta_{0}\cdot\bigl\|\pi_{\mathsf{I}}(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})-h\otimes g\bigr\|^{2}-L_{\sf B}(\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}+\bar{h}\otimes g)\right].

(181)

Therefore, (
180
) is minimized if and only if

\displaystyle\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}=\pi_{\mathsf{B}}\Bigl(\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\Bigr),

(182)

and (
181
) is minimized if and only if

\displaystyle\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}+\bar{h}\otimes g=\pi_{\mathsf{B}}\Bigl(\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\Bigr),\qquad h\otimes g=\md_{\leq k}\left(\pi_{\mathsf{I}}\Bigl(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\Bigr)\right).

(183)

Hence, the common solution of (
182
)
and (
183
) is

\displaystyle\bar{f}\otimes\bar{g}=\pi_{\mathsf{B}}\Bigl(\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\Bigr),\qquad\bar{h}\otimes g=0,\qquad h\otimes g=\md_{\leq k}\left(\pi_{\mathsf{I}}\Bigl(\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\Bigr)\right),

(184)

which is equivalent to (
97
). Finally, from Proposition
41
, this is also the solution that maximizes the nested H-score (
96
). ∎

C.20
Proof of Theorem
36

We first introduce two useful facts.

Fact 6

(
Cover and Thomas 2006
)
.

Given
m
samples
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m}
i.i.d. generated from
Q_{Z}\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{Z}}
, th probability of observing
\{Z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}=\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
, denoted by
\mathbb{P}\left\{\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m};Q_{Z}\right\}
, satisfies

\displaystyle-\log\mathbb{P}\left\{\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m};Q_{Z}\right\}=m\left[H(\hat{P}_{Z})+D(\hat{P}_{Z}\|Q_{Z})\right]
,
where
\hat{P}_{Z}
is the empirical distribution of
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
.

Fact 7

(
Huang et al. 2024
)
.

Given a reference distribution
R\in\relint(\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{Z}})
. Then, for
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{Z}}
with
\|\tilde{\ell}_{P;R}\|=O(\epsilon)
,
\|\tilde{\ell}_{Q;R}\|=O(\epsilon)
, we have

\displaystyle D(P\|Q)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|\tilde{\ell}_{P;R}-\tilde{\ell}_{Q;R}\|^{2}+o(\epsilon^{2}).

Note that since the data from three different datasets are generated independently, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{0},\mathscr{D}_{1},\mathscr{D}_{2};Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{0};Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\right\}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{1};Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\right\}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{2};Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{0};Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\right\}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{1};Q_{X_{1},Y}\right\}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{2};Q_{X_{2},Y}\right\}.

Therefore, from Fact
6
,

\displaystyle-\log\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{0},\mathscr{D}_{1},\mathscr{D}_{2};Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad=-\log\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{0};Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\right\}-\log\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{1};Q_{X_{1},Y}\right\}-\log\mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{2};Q_{X_{2},Y}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad=n_{0}\cdot D\bigl(\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\big\|Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\bigr)+n_{1}\cdot D\bigl(\hat{P}_{X_{1},Y}^{(1)}\big\|Q_{X_{1},Y}\bigr)+n_{2}\cdot D\bigl(\hat{P}_{X_{2},Y}^{(2)}\big\|Q_{X_{2},Y}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+n_{0}\cdot H(\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})+n_{1}\cdot H(\hat{P}_{X_{1},Y}^{(1)})+n_{2}\cdot H(\hat{P}_{X_{2},Y}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\qquad=n\cdot L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})+n_{0}\cdot H(\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})+n_{1}\cdot H(\hat{P}_{X_{1},Y}^{(1)})+n_{2}\cdot H(\hat{P}_{X_{2},Y}^{(2)})

where the second equality follows from Fact
6
, and where we have defined

\displaystyle L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})\triangleq\eta_{0}\cdot D\bigl(\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\big\|Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\bigr)+\eta_{1}\cdot D\bigl(\hat{P}_{X_{1},Y}^{(1)}\big\|Q_{X_{1},Y}\bigr)+\eta_{2}\cdot D\bigl(\hat{P}_{X_{2},Y}^{(2)}\big\|Q_{X_{2},Y}\bigr).

Hence, we can rewrite the maximum likelihood solution
P^{(\mathrm{ML})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
as

\displaystyle P^{(\mathrm{ML})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}

\displaystyle=\argmax_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\penalty\ \mathbb{P}\left\{\mathscr{D}_{0},\mathscr{D}_{1},\mathscr{D}_{2};Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\right\}=\argmin_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}).

From
L(R_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})=O(\epsilon^{2})
, we obtain
\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|=O(\epsilon),\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(1)}_{X_{1},Y}}\bigr\|=O(\epsilon),\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(2)}_{X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|=O(\epsilon)
. By definition, we have
L({P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})<L(R_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})=O(\epsilon^{2})
, which implies that
\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|=O(\epsilon)
.

We first consider
Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
with

\displaystyle\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}-\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|\leq\epsilon.

(185)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|\leq\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}-\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|+\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|=O(\epsilon),

(186)

and it follows from Fact
7
that

\displaystyle L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})

\displaystyle=\eta_{0}\cdot D\bigl(\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\big\|Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\bigr)+\eta_{1}\cdot D\bigl(\hat{P}_{X_{1},Y}^{(1)}\big\|Q_{X_{1},Y}\bigr)+\eta_{2}\cdot D\bigl(\hat{P}_{X_{2},Y}^{(2)}\big\|Q_{X_{2},Y}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\eta_{0}\cdot\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(0)}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}-\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}+\eta_{1}\cdot\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(1)}_{X_{1},Y}}-\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\eta_{2}\cdot\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\hat{P}^{(2)}_{X_{2},Y}}-\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|^{2}\Bigr)+o(\epsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot L({Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})+o(\epsilon^{2}).

(187)

Therefore, for
Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
that satisfies (
185
), the minimum value of
L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})
is achieved by
Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}=P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}+o(\epsilon)
.

Now we consider the case of
Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
with

\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}-\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|>\epsilon
. Let
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}-\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|<1
and define

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}\triangleq\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}+(1-\epsilon^{\prime})\cdot P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}.

(188)

Then, we have
\tilde{\ell}_{\bar{Q}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}=\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}+(1-\epsilon^{\prime})\cdot\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}
, which implies

\displaystyle\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{\bar{Q}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}-\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|=\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\bigl\|\tilde{\ell}_{Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}-\tilde{\ell}_{P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}}\bigr\|=\epsilon.

(189)

As a result, we can apply the same analysis on
\bar{Q}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
and obtain [cf. (
187
)]

\displaystyle L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(\bar{Q}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot L({\bar{Q}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})+o(\epsilon^{2}).

(190)

Hence, we obtain from (
189
) that

\displaystyle L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(\bar{Q}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})>L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot L({P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}})+o(\epsilon^{2})

(191)

for
\epsilon
sufficiently small.

In addition, since
L^{(\mathrm{ML})}
is convex, it follows from Jensen’s inequality that (cf. (
188
))

\displaystyle L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(\bar{Q}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})+(1-\epsilon^{\prime})\cdot L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}).

(192)

As a result, from (
191
) and (
192
) we have
L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})>L^{(\mathrm{ML})}(P^{(\mathrm{est})}_{X_{1},X_{2},Y})
.

Combining both cases of
Q_{X_{1},X_{2},Y}
, we obtain (
99
). ∎

C.21
Proof of Proposition
37

It suffices to establish that there exist
\alpha\colon\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\beta\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}
, such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{X};U}(\underline{x},u)

\displaystyle=\alpha(u)\cdot\left[\tanh\left(2w\cdot\varphi(\underline{x})+b_{U}\right)-\tanh(b_{U})\right],

(193a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{Y};V}(\underline{y},v)

\displaystyle=\beta(v)\cdot\left[\tanh\left(2w\cdot\varphi(\underline{y})+b_{V}\right)-\tanh(b_{V})\right].

(193b)

To see this, note that from the Markov relation
\underline{X}-U-V-\underline{Y}
, we have

\displaystyle P_{\underline{X},\underline{Y}}(\underline{x},\underline{y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U},v\in\mathcal{V}}P_{\underline{X}|U}(\underline{x}|u)\cdot P_{\underline{Y}|V}(\underline{y}|v)\cdot P_{U,V}(u,v)

\displaystyle=P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})P_{\underline{Y}}(\underline{y})\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U},v\in\mathcal{V}}P_{U,V}(u,v)\cdot(1+\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{X};U}(\underline{x},u))\cdot(1+\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{Y};V}(\underline{y},v))

\displaystyle=P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})P_{\underline{Y}}(\underline{y})\left(1+\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U},v\in\mathcal{V}}P_{U,V}(u,v)\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{X};U}(\underline{x},u)\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{Y};V}(\underline{y},v)\right),

where to obtain the last equality we have used the fact that

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U}}P_{U}(u)\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{X};U}(\underline{x},u)=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}P_{V}(v)\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{Y};V}(\underline{y},v)=0.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{X};\underline{Y}}(\underline{x},\underline{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{\underline{X},\underline{Y}}(\underline{x},\underline{y})}{P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})P_{\underline{Y}}(\underline{y})}-1

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{U},v\in\mathcal{V}}P_{U,V}(u,v)\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{X};U}(\underline{x},u)\cdot\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{Y};V}(\underline{y},v)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\alpha(U)\beta(V)\right]\cdot\left[\tanh\left(2w\cdot\varphi(\underline{x})+b_{U}\right)-\tanh(b_{U})\right]\cdot\left[\tanh\left(2w\cdot\varphi(\underline{y})+b_{V}\right)-\tanh(b_{V})\right],

which gives (
105
).

It remains only to establish (
193
). For symmetry, we consider only (
193a
). To begin,
by definition, we have

\displaystyle P_{\underline{X}|U=u}(\underline{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{l-1}\left[q_{u}^{(1-\delta_{x_{i}x_{i+1}})}(1-q_{u})^{\delta_{x_{i}x_{i+1}}}\right],

which implies

\displaystyle\log P_{\underline{X}|U=u}(\underline{x})=\log\frac{1}{2}+\log q_{u}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}(1-\delta_{x_{i}x_{i+1}})+\log(1-q_{u})\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\delta_{x_{i}x_{i+1}}.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\log{P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})}-\log{P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\log\frac{q_{1}}{q_{0}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}(1-\delta_{x_{i}x_{i+1}})+\log\frac{1-q_{1}}{1-q_{0}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\delta_{x_{i}x_{i+1}}\right]

\displaystyle=\log\frac{q_{1}}{q_{0}}\cdot\left(\frac{l-1}{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\delta_{x_{i}x_{i+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=2w\cdot\varphi(\underline{x}).

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})-P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})-P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}{P_{U}(1)\cdot P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})+P_{U}(0)\cdot P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{P_{U}(0)}\cdot\frac{\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}-1}{\frac{P_{U}(1)}{P_{U}(0)}\cdot\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2P_{U}(0)P_{U}(1)}\cdot\left[\frac{\frac{P_{U}(1)}{P_{U}(0)}\cdot\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}-1}{\frac{P_{U}(1)}{P_{U}(0)}\cdot\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}+1}-\frac{\frac{P_{U}(1)}{P_{U}(0)}-1}{\frac{P_{U}(1)}{P_{U}(0)}+1}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2P_{U}(0)P_{U}(1)}\cdot\left[\tanh\left(2w\cdot\varphi(\underline{x})+b_{U}\right)-\tanh(b_{U})\right],

where to obtain the last equality we have used the fact that
\frac{t-1}{t+1}=\tanh\left(\frac{1}{2}\log t\right)
.

Hence, with
u^{\prime}=1-u
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{\underline{X};U}(\underline{x},u)

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{\underline{X},U}(\underline{x},u)-P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})P_{U}(u)}{P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})P_{U}(u)}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=u}(\underline{x})-P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=u}(\underline{x})-P_{U}(u)P_{\underline{X}|U=u}(\underline{x})-P_{U}(u^{\prime})P_{\underline{X}|U=u^{\prime}}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})}

\displaystyle=P_{U}(u^{\prime})\cdot\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=u}(\underline{x})-P_{\underline{X}|U=u^{\prime}}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})}

\displaystyle=P_{U}(u^{\prime})\cdot(-1)^{u+1}\cdot\frac{P_{\underline{X}|U=1}(\underline{x})-P_{\underline{X}|U=0}(\underline{x})}{P_{\underline{X}}(\underline{x})}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{u+1}}{2P_{U}(u)}\cdot\left[\tanh\left(2w\cdot\varphi(\underline{x})+b_{U}\right)-\tanh(b_{U})\right],

which gives (
193a
) as desired, with
\alpha(u)=\frac{(-1)^{u+1}}{2P_{U}(u)}
. ∎

Appendix D
Implementation Details of Experiments

We implement our experiments in Python 3
(
Van Rossum and Drake 2009
)
, where we use
the
PyTorch

(
Paszke et al. 2019
)
library for neural network training and use the
Matplotlib

(
Hunter 2007
)
library for plotting. We also make use of
NumPy

(
Harris et al. 2020
)
and
SciPy

(
Virtanen et al. 2020
)
for the computation.

In the experiments, we apply Adam
(
Kingma and Ba 2015
)
as the optimizer with the default parameters: a learning rate of
10^{-3}
,
\beta_{1}=0.9,\beta_{2}=0.999
, and
\epsilon=10^{-8}
. For each MLP (multilayer perceptron) used in the experiments, we set the activation function to be the softplus function
x\mapsto\log(1+e^{x})
, which are applied to all layers except the output layer.

It is worth mentioning that our choices of network architectures, optimizers and hyperparameters are not optimized with respect to the used data distributions. It is possible to further optimize such choices to improve the performance or convergence.

D.1
Learning Maximal Correlation Functions

We first introduce the implementation details for Section
7.1
, where the goal is to learn maximal correlation functions for different data. The corresponding learning objective is the nested H-score (
38
), which are maximized during the training.

D.1.1
Implementation of Section
7.1.1

We set
|\mathcal{X}|=8,|\mathcal{Y}|=6
, and feature dimension
k=3
. To generate the discrete distributions
P_{X,Y}
, we draw
(|\mathcal{X}|\cdot|\mathcal{Y}|)
i.i.d. numbers from
\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]
and divide each number by their sum. We then use the resulting
|\mathcal{X}|\times|\mathcal{Y}|
table as the values for the probability mass function
P_{X,Y}
. To ensure reproducible results, we set the random seed of
NumPy
to
20\,230\,606
in the generating process.

Then, we generate
N=30\,000
training sample pairs of
(X,Y)
from
P_{X,Y}
, then apply one-hot encoding such that the inputs are represented as
|\mathcal{X}|
and
|\mathcal{Y}|
dimensional vectors. Then, we use two one-layer linear networks as the feature extractors
f
and
g
.

We train the networks with a minibatch size of 128 for 100 epochs. Then, we obtain the estimated
f_{i}^{*}
,
g_{i}^{*}
, and
\sigma_{i}
by applying (
40
) and compare them with corresponding theoretical values, which we compute from the SVD of corresponding CDM matrix [cf. (
120
)], with the results shown in Figure
14
. Note that since
f_{i}^{*}\otimes g_{i}^{*}=(-f_{i}^{*})\otimes(-g_{i}^{*})
, both
(f_{i}^{*},g_{i}^{*})
and
(-f_{i}^{*},-g_{i}^{*})
are the optimal feature pairs. For the sake of presentation, we applied a sign modification before the plotting.

D.1.2
Implementation of Section
7.1.2

In this experiment, we first generate
N=50\,000
samples of
(X,Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
for training, to learn
k=2
dimensional features
f
and
g
. We use two MLPs of the same architecture as the feature extractors for
f
and
g
. Specifically, each MLP is with three layers, where the dimensions for all intermediate features, from input to output, are:
\text{input}=1
– 32 – 32 –
2=\text{output}
. We then train the networks with a minibatch size of 256 for 100 epochs and use the learned features for estimation tasks, as demonstrated in Section
7.1.2
.

D.1.3
Implementation of Section
7.1.3

In this experiment, we set
k=1
. To extract
f
and
g
from input sequences
\underline{X}
and
\underline{Y}
, we use one-dimensional convolutional neural networks as the feature extractors, which are used in sentence classifications
(
Kim 2014
;
Zhang and Wallace 2017
)
. In particular,
f
and
g
are of the same architecture, composed of an embedding (linear) layer, a 1 dimensional convolutional layer, an average pooling layer, and a fully connected (linear) layer.

We use feature extractor
f
as an example to illustrate the processing of sequential data. First, we represent
\underline{x}
sequence as a one-hot encoded list, i.e., each
x_{i}\in\{(1,0)^{\mathrm{T}},(0,1)^{\mathrm{T}}\}
. Then, the embedding layer maps each
x_{i}
to a 4-dimensional vector. The one-dimensional convolutional layer then processes the length-
l
list of embedded 4-dimensional vectors, by 32 convolutional kernels of size 4. We then activate the convolution results by the ReLU function
x\mapsto\max\{x,0\}
. The output from each convolutional kernel is further averaged by the average pooling layer, leading to a 32 dimensional feature, with each dimension corresponding to a convolutional kernel.
Finally, we feed the 32 dimensional feature to the fully connected layer and generate
k=1
dimensional output.

Then, we train the feature extractors
f
and
g
with a minibatch size of 128 for 100 epochs. The learned features are shown in Section
7.1.3
.

D.2
Learning With Orthogonality Constraints

In this experiment, we use the same dataset generated in Appendix
D.1.2
. We set
{\bar{k}}=k=1
, i.e., we learn one-dimensional feature
f
from
X
orthogonal to given one-dimensional
\bar{f}
. To this end, we use three MLPs of the same architecture as the feature extractors for
\bar{g},f,g
, with dimensions

\text{input}=1
– 32 – 32 –
1=\text{output}
. We then train the networks with a minibatch size of 256 for 100 epochs to maximize the nested H-score restricted to
\bar{f}=\phi
[cf. (
52
)], for
\phi(x)=x
and
\phi(x)=x^{2}
, respectively.

D.3
Learning With Side Information

We set
|\mathcal{X}|=8,|\mathcal{S}|=|\mathcal{Y}|=3
, and generate
P_{X,S,Y}
in a manner similar to Appendix
D.1.1
, with the same random seed.
Then, we generate
N=50\,000
training samples of
(X,S,Y)
triples. In our implementation, we set
{\bar{k}}=|\mathcal{S}|-1=2
and
k=1
, and set feature extractors
\bar{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\,2},\bar{g}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\,2}
,
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}
,
g\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}}
as corresponding one-layer linear networks with one-hot encoded inputs. In particular, we convert each
(s,y)
to one unique one-hot vector of in
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}|\cdot|\mathcal{Y}|}
as the input to the network
g
. Then, we train these feature extractors on the training set with a minibatch size of 256 for 100 epochs, to maximize the nested H-score configured by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{MC}}
[cf. (
59
)].

For comparison, we train a multihead network shown in Figure
11
on the same dataset, with the same minibatch size and epochs. The feature
f
is again implemented by a one-layer linear network. In particular, we maximize the log-likelihood function (
70
) to learn the corresponding feature and weights. Then, we convert the weights to
g\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}}
via the correspondence [cf. (
69
)]
g(s,y)=G_{s}(1,y)
.
The comparison between features learned from two approaches are shown in Figure
20
. For the sake of presentation, we have normalized the features before plotting, such that
f
and each
g(s,\cdot)
are zero-mean, and unit variance with respect to
\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X})
and
\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{Y})
, respectively.

D.4
Multimodal Learning With Missing Modalities

D.4.1
Implementation of Section
7.4.1

We first generate
N=50\,000
triples of
(X_{1},X_{2},Y)
for training. In implementing the algorithm, we
{\bar{k}}=k=1
. To represent
\bar{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{X}}
, we set each
\bar{f}^{(i)}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
as an MLP with
dimensions
\text{input}=1
– 32 – 32 –
1=\text{output}
. To represent
f
, we use an MLP with dimensions
\text{input}=2
– 32 – 32 –
1=\text{output}
with the input set to
X_{1}\mathbin{+\mkern-10.0mu+}X_{2}=(X_{1},X_{2})^{\mathrm{T}}
. Since
Y
is discrete, we use one-layer linear network as
\bar{g}
and
g
, where the inputs are one-hot encoded
Y
. Therefore, both
\bar{g}
and
g
are with one linear layer, taking
|\mathcal{Y}|=2
dimensional input and outputting one-dimensional feature.

We then train these feature extractors on the training set with a minibatch size of 256 for 100 epochs, to maximize the nested H-score configured by
\mathcal{C}_{\sf BI}
[cf. (
78
)].
The learned features are then shown in Figure
21
.

For the prediction problem with missing modality, we use two MLPs to represent conditional expectation operators
\phi_{1}\triangleq\tau_{{1}}(\bar{f}^{(2)})
and
\phi_{2}\triangleq\tau_{{2}}(\bar{f}^{(1)})
, each with dimensions
\text{input}=1
– 32 – 32 –
1=\text{output}
. These two networks are optimized by minimizing the corresponding mean square error. For example, we train
\phi_{1}
by minimizing

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\bar{f}^{(2)}(X_{2})-\phi_{1}(X_{1})\right)^{2}\right]
over all parameters in
\phi_{1}
network. The training of
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
is with minibatch size of 256, and 100 epochs.

D.4.2
Implementation of Section
7.4.2

We generate
N=50\,000
triples of
(X_{1},X_{2},Y)
from (
106
) and (
111
), and adopt the decomposition (
89
) on each triple. This gives three pairwise datasets with samples of
(X_{1},X_{2})
,
(X_{1},Y)
,
(X_{2},Y)
, where each dataset has
N
sample pairs. Then, we adopt the same setting of networks to represent one-dimensional
\bar{f}^{(1)},\bar{f}^{(2)}
and
g
.

We then train these feature extractors on the three datasets for
100 epochs with a minibatch size of 256, to maximize
\mathscr{H}(\bar{f},\bar{g})
.
Here, we compute
\mathscr{H}(\bar{f},\bar{g})
based on the minibatches from the three pairwise datasets according to (
93
).

To solve the prediction problem with missing modality, we use the same network architectures and training settings to learn
\tau_{{1}}(\bar{f}^{(2)})
and
\tau_{{2}}(\bar{f}^{(1)})
, as introduced in Appendix
D.4.1
.
